### Com-Affiliated Hackers Hijack Cloud Accounts via Vishing for Data Theft and Extortion
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T06:37:06Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware, apt_intrusion, cloud
- Summary: Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 has disclosed that it's tracking and has responded to "numerous waves of vishing attacks" orchestrated by Com-affiliated threat actors since August 2026 that have led to data theft and extortion attempts. The attacks have targeted financial services, healthcare, and legal services located in the U.S., the U.K., and Australia. Other targets include construction,
- Technical details: Com-affiliated hackers use vishing, session hijacking, and unauthorized MFA enrollment to steal corporate cloud data and extort victims.

Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 has disclosed that it's tracking and has responded to "numerous waves of vishing attacks" orchestrated by Com-affiliated threat actors since August 2026 that have led to data theft and extortion attempts.

The attacks have targeted financial services, healthcare, and legal services located in the U.S., the U.K., and Australia. Other targets include construction, high-technology, transportation, manufacturing, pharmaceuticals, insurance, and real estate sectors.

"The victims share a common profile: they hold large volumes of high-impact corporate data where exposure carries severe regulatory, legal, or market consequences," Unit 42 said . "These attackers targeted financial services organizations about twice as often as healthcare organizations in August 2026, but healthcare overtook financial services in September."

The activity has been attributed to a number of threat clusters , including -

CL-CRI-1116 (aka Redact, formerly BlackFile), first observed in January 2026

CL-CRI-1192 (aka Helix and UNC6671), first observed in June 2026

CL-CRI-1264 (aka Vortex), first observed in September 2026

CL-CRI-1274 (aka Cinder), first observed in September 2026

Intrusions typically commence with voice phishing, with the attackers posing as IT staff to resolve a purported authentication issue and direct the victim to a phishing page personalized to their organization.

Once the AiTM kit captures credentials and session tokens, the attackers quickly enroll their own multi-factor authentication (MFA) devices, abuse cloud utilities and, in a few instances, deploy malicious browser extensions to exfiltrate data and extort victims.

The harvested authentication data is used to authenticate to victim environments, typically by routing such requests through residential proxy networks and commercial VPNs. The adversaries then seek to establish multi-layered persistence by registering their MFA devices, generating cloud infrastructure temporary credentials, authorizing unapproved third-party OAuth applications; enumerate sensitive data; evade defenses by modifying mailbox rules and clearing security alert events; and harvest data from cloud platforms.

"Attackers associated with CL-CRI-1264 establish initial access through vishing, where they pose as internal IT support and direct employees to servers using subdomains tailored to each targeted organization," Unit 42 said.

"These servers act as adversary-in-the-middle (AitM) proxies that pass user credentials and multi-factor authentication (MFA) approvals to the attackers in real time. Attackers have targeted employees on both their corporate workstations and personal mobile devices."

CL-CRI-1264 has also been observed enrolling unauthorized MFA devices within minutes of authenticating to establish persistence, often reusing a single device named Pixel 10a across compromised accounts. The attack chains involve automated data exfiltration using Python scripts to harvest information from Microsoft 365 mailbox, OneDrive, and SharePoint.

Once data is exfiltrated, the threat actor sends Microsoft Teams messages directly to employees at victim organizations, claiming data theft and instructing them to visit a TOR-hosted ransom negotiation site.

The second new cluster to emerge around the same time is CL-CRI-1274, which has also leaned on vishing for initial access and leveraged the Microsoft Graph software development kit (SDK) to collect and exfiltrate data from the victim's SharePoint and OneDrive environments.

"Afterwards, the attackers used a compromised account to send extortion messages internally via email and Microsoft Teams, ultimately directing recipients to a Tor-hosted negotiation site," Unit 42 said.

The cybersecurity company said it also observed the CL-CRI-1192 attackers using a malicious browser extension named "Google Chrome" that's said to have been sideloaded onto the browser after enabling Developer Mode. The add-on performs a number of malicious actions, such as -
- What is it? A collaboration and document-management platform.
- What does it do? It helps organisations store, share and manage documents, intranet pages and team information.

### WordPress Blocks Six Backdoored WP Connect Plugins Before Malicious Releases Go Live
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T06:21:12Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-135202 | Topic signals: malware_botnet
- Summary: WordPress has removed six WordPress plugins developed by WP Connect after they were backdoored on November 22, 2026, to enable arbitrary code execution on sites that have them installed, following a suspected compromise of the developer's account. The WordPress plugins team has proactively detected and blocked the threat before it could be officially released. Visiting each plugin listing now
- Technical details: WordPress blocked malicious releases of six WP Connect plugins containing backdoors designed to enable arbitrary code execution.

WordPress has removed six WordPress plugins developed by WP Connect after they were backdoored on November 22, 2026, to enable arbitrary code execution on sites that have them installed, following a suspected compromise of the developer's account.

The WordPress plugins team has proactively detected and blocked the threat before it could be officially released. Visiting each plugin listing now serves the message: "This plugin has been closed as of November 22, 2026, and is not available for download. This closure is permanent."

The names of the affected plugins and the malicious versions are listed below -

These plugins have been collectively installed on more than 3,600 WordPress sites, according to WordPress security company Wordfence. The supply chain compromise is being tracked as CVE-2026-135202 (CVSS score: 9.8). It remains unpatched.

"Each release silently loaded the same remote-control module, which phones home to an attacker-controlled server, executes any PHP code it is sent, and exposes a full command set for running shell commands, stealing password hashes, and planting webshells," security researcher Chloe Chamberland said .

Specifically, the plugins come with a backdoor module ("includes/airwp-online.php") that runs on every request, sends site information (site URL, PHP, WordPress, and OS versions, and the server user) to a hard-coded command-and-control server ("tascredit-pay[.]com"), and runs PHP code returned by the server via eval().

Dump the users table including login, email address, and password hashes

Drop a web shell into the uploads directory or to a path of the attacker's choosing

Register an hourly WP-Cron hook that runs attacker-supplied code

As a result, an unauthenticated attacker in control of the site can execute arbitrary code on the server and take complete control, facilitating credential theft and persistence through web shells and cron hooks that outlast the plugin removal.

Because the malicious versions were never released and blocked due to WordPress's new cooldown protections as part of its Protect The Shire initiative, sites running these plugins are unlikely to be affected. However, it's essential that site owners check their websites to make sure that they are not running one of the compromised versions and remove them as soon as possible.

"Unlike a typical vulnerability, this was not a coding mistake, and there is no patch: the malicious code was added deliberately and committed to each plugin's WordPress.org repository," Chamberland said. "Taken together, this strongly suggests that the developer's WordPress.org account was compromised and used to push the malicious releases, rather than that the vendor shipped this code intentionally."

The disclosure comes as URLscan.io revealed that an unspecified WordPress site had been infected by multiple cybercriminal campaigns, with attackers attempting to disable or remove scripts belonging to competing threat actors.

The site carried "thirteen separate ClickFix injections from three distinct kits [LOM, ErrTraffic, and Macfinger], alongside seven further malicious loaders," it said . "Several of the injections attempt to remove each other's code and overlays, and one injection disables another through a JavaScript naming collision. Five of the thirteen injections resolve their next-stage infrastructure from Polygon smart contracts, a technique known as EtherHiding ."
- What is it? A content management system (CMS) used to build websites.
- What does it do? It lets site owners publish pages, posts and manage website features through themes and plugins.

### USN-9194-1: libxrender vulnerabilities
- Source: Ubuntu Security Notices | Published: 2026-11-25T02:38:24Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2016-7949, CVE-2016-7950, CVE-2026-88807 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Adam Bedard discovered that libXrender incorrectly handled certain responses from an X server, resulting in a heap buffer overflow. If a user were tricked into connecting to a malicious X server, an attacker could possibly use this issue to cause applications using libXrender to crash, resulting in a denial of service, or execute arbitrary code. (CVE-2026-88807) Tobias Stoeckmann discovered that libXrender did not correctly validate lengths in responses from an X server. An attacker controlling an X server could possibly use these issues to cause applications using libXrender to crash, resulting in a denial of service, or execute arbitrary code. This update provides the fixes for Ubuntu 14.04 LTS. (CVE-2016-7949, CVE-2016-7950)

If a user were tricked into connecting to a malicious X server, an attacker could possibly use this issue to cause applications using libXrender to crash, resulting in a denial of service, or execute arbitrary code. ( CVE-2026-88807 )

Tobias Stoeckmann discovered that libXrender did not correctly validate lengths in responses from an X server. An attacker controlling an X server could possibly use these issues to cause applications using libXrender to crash, resulting in a denial of service, or execute arbitrary code. This update provides the fixes for Ubuntu 14.04 LTS. ( CVE-2016-7949 , CVE-2016-7950 )

In general, a standard system update will make all the necessary changes.

The problem can be corrected by updating your system to the following package versions:

### NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633
- Source: Palo Alto Unit 42 | Published: 2026-11-24T23:00:14Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-123633, CVE-2026-123634 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Technical analysis of CVE-2026-123633, an NGINX HTTP/2 use-after-free flaw, with details on affected versions, sanitizer tests, and patch guidance. The post NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633 appeared first on Unit 42 .
- Technical details: Technical analysis of CVE-2026-123633, an NGINX HTTP/2 use-after-free flaw, with details on affected versions, sanitizer tests, and patch guidance.

CVE-2026-123633 is a use-after-free vulnerability in NGINX HTTP/2 that may let an unauthenticated attacker cause a denial of service. In the CVE record, F5 says code execution may be possible when address space layout randomization (ASLR) is disabled or bypassed.

CVE-2026-123633 affects NGINX servers that accept HTTP/2 client connections and proxy requests to a backend when free worker connections run low. A fragmented request can leave an active connection marked reusable. Under connection pressure, NGINX can close that connection and free memory the active request still uses.

F5's CVE record scores the issue 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0. The NGINX security advisories page labels the issue medium and lists versions 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected. F5 credits memN0ps (Palo Alto Networks Unit 42) among the reporters.

NGINX 1.30.7 on the stable branch and 1.31.9 on the mainline branch contain the fix. F5 also lists affected NGINX Plus releases .

Administrators should review HTTP/2 reverse-proxy exposure and upgrade to a fixed NGINX release or a vendor package containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix.

Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products and services:

Organizations can access tailored adversary intelligence, direct analyst consultations, and specialized exposure tracking through Unit 42 Threat Intel Services and its Deep and Dark Web Service .

If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team .

While researching NGINX, I independently found the HTTP/2 connection-state defect now tracked as CVE-2026-123633.

An unauthenticated client can send a fragmented HTTP/2 request that leaves an active connection marked as reusable. When the server resolves a hostname during the request and free connection slots run low, NGINX can reclaim that connection mid-request and free memory its DNS resolver is still using.

I reproduced the use-after-free with AddressSanitizer (ASan) on builds of unmodified NGINX 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 source. The NGINX security advisories page lists 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected and 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 as fixed releases.

I reported the HTTP/2 use-after-free privately to F5. F5's CVE record scores it 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0 and 8.1 High under CVSS 3.1. The NGINX security advisories page labels it Medium.

In my ASan test on a Debian guest, ASan stopped NGINX at the first read from freed DNS lookup memory.

The resolver path in my test stems from the HTTP/2 connection bug tracked as CVE-2026-123633. CVE-2026-123634 is a separate DNS resolver vulnerability fixed in the same release.

I also found a memory disclosure vulnerability that leaked a libc pointer. I used the address to calculate the libc base and work around ASLR when chaining the disclosure with CVE-2026-123633. I reported this additional bug to F5 as a separate issue. From an attacker VM, I achieved pre-authentication remote code execution on a target VM as the non-root NGINX worker. The chain required a non-default NGINX configuration, and some attempts did not open a shell.

F5 assessed the memory disclosure as a behavior limited to a non-typical configuration and has not assigned it a CVE at the time of writing, so I am leaving out its details and the exploit steps.

NGINX marks a connection reusable when it can close that connection under pressure. Stream creation should clear the reusable mark once a request starts, but ngx_http_v2_create_stream() leaves it set in affected releases.

When a client sends only part of an HTTP/2 HEADERS frame header, ngx_http_v2_handle_connection() marks the connection reusable and returns early. The code below shows that decision:
- What is it? Web-server and reverse-proxy software.
- What does it do? It serves websites and can route traffic to application servers, balance load and handle TLS connections.
- Fix / remediation: Administrators should review HTTP/2 reverse-proxy exposure and upgrade to a fixed NGINX release or a vendor package containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix. CVE-2026-123634 is a separate DNS resolver vulnerability fixed in the same release.

On October 21, 2026, Node.js announced the availability of fixes for multiple vulnerabilities across the following supported release lines: 22.x, 24.x, and 26.x.

These vulnerabilities impact critical operations such as the Brotli decoder, DNS resolution, and TLS certificate validation. The vulnerabilities pose risks for remote code execution and traffic interception. For more information, see Node.js Security Release .

The following vulnerabilities have a Security Impact Rating (SIR) of High and are addressed in this advisory:

CVE-2026-111497 - Brotli decoder out-of-bounds write; potential remote code execution

CVE-2026-105482 - DNS resolution truncation involving embedded NUL characters

CVE-2026-105483 - TLS hostname validation bypass using fullwidth IP digits

CVE-2026-106069 - TLS hostname validation bypass with noncanonical IP addresses

For information about Splunk products and offerings, including AppDynamics, see Vulnerabilities in Node.js (CVE-2026-111497, CVE-2026-105482, CVE-2026-105483, CVE-2026-106069) on Splunk Products - October 2026

This investigation was expanded to cover Brotli outside of Node.js on October 30, 2026. The Brotli decoder flaw (CVE-2026-111497), which is in the Google Brotli C decoder, is often bundled with or linked to other software, so products beyond Node.js may be affected. For software that may be affected, see Broccoli2Shell .

Important: If a product is not listed within this advisory, Cisco's review indicates that the product is not affected by these vulnerabilities.

This advisory is available at the following link: https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/content/CiscoSecurityAdvisory/cisco-sa-oct2026-node-js-Jj3SQ19u

### USN-9193-1: PCRE vulnerability
- Source: Ubuntu Security Notices | Published: 2026-11-24T21:11:52Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Yunho Kim discovered that PCRE incorrectly handled memory when processing certain regular expressions with JIT compilation enabled. An attacker could possibly use this issue to cause applications using PCRE to expose sensitive information.
- Technical details: Ubuntu is an open source software operating system that runs from the desktop, to the cloud, to all your internet connected things.

Thank you for contacting us. A member of our team will be in touch shortly. Close

Thank you for signing up for our newsletter! In these regular emails you will find the latest updates about Ubuntu and upcoming events where you can meet our team. Close

Your preferences have been successfully updated. Close notification

PCRE could be made to expose sensitive information if it received specially crafted input.

Yunho Kim discovered that PCRE incorrectly handled memory when processing certain regular expressions with JIT compilation enabled. An attacker could possibly use this issue to cause applications using PCRE to expose sensitive information.

### RHSA-2026:102952: Important: cifs-utils security update
- Source: Red Hat Security Advisories | Published: 2026-11-24T20:55:11Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-104715 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: An update for cifs-utils is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9. Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.
- Technical details: An update for cifs-utils is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

The SMB/CIFS protocol is a standard file sharing protocol widely deployed on Microsoft Windows machines. The cifs-utils packages contain tools for mounting shares on Linux using the SMB/CIFS protocol. The tools in this package work in conjunction with support in the kernel to allow one to mount a SMB/CIFS share onto a client and use it as if it were a standard Linux file system.

cifs-utils: mount.cifs credential-file option injection allows local privilege boundary bypass (CVE-2026-104715)

For more details about the security issue(s), including the impact, a CVSS score, acknowledgments, and other related information, refer to the CVE page(s) listed in the References section.

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:

Red Hat Enterprise Linux for Power, little endian 9 ppc64le

Red Hat CodeReady Linux Builder for Power, little endian 9 ppc64le

Red Hat CodeReady Linux Builder for IBM z Systems 9 s390x

BZ - 2545023 - CVE-2026-104715 cifs-utils: mount.cifs credential-file option injection allows local privilege boundary bypass

https://access.redhat.com/security/updates/classification/#important

The Red Hat security contact is secalert@redhat.com . More contact details at https://access.redhat.com/security/team/contact/ .

### Microsoft: China’s Salt Typhoon hackers are using Artex
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-25T21:18:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: An AI tool implicated in several high-profile breaches is rapidly being integrated into the hacking suites of groups from China and North Korea, Microsoft said.
- Business background / implications: An AI tool implicated in several high-profile breaches is rapidly being integrated into the hacking suites of groups from China and North Korea, Microsoft said.

State-backed hacking groups in China and North Korea are using a powerful AI hacking tool in attacks, according to a new report from Microsoft.

Microsoft explained that state-sponsored and financially motivated hackers are using Artex, an AI-powered platform that can automate multiple phases of cyber intrusions at machine speed.

The study emerged hours after The New York Times and NextGov reported that Artex was allegedly used in attacks on the CIA, the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence.

Microsoft said on Wednesday that Chinese state-backed groups Crystal Typhoon and Salt Typhoon have integrated Artex into their suite of tools, using it for “network reconnaissance, credential attacks, and web application exploitation.”

“In the past few weeks, we’ve reported several distinct campaigns that abused Artex to search public repositories for exposed secrets, enable supply chain compromise, and scan internet-facing systems for exploitation,” Microsoft explained.

Salt Typhoon is a prolific Chinese hacking group that U.S. agencies believe was behind the breach of at least nine telecommunications giants in 2024. The hack alarmed White House officials in 2024 because it allegedly gave China’s hackers access to communications from the phones of President Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance as well as several Democratic leaders.

Salt Typhoon was also accused of attacking several European governments and critical infrastructure organizations globally .

Artex was released in July, and according to Microsoft, it has been steadily added to the arsenal of multiple North Korean and Chinese groups.

Artex allows threat actors to define specific goals and use AI agents to attack targets. Microsoft said Artex “can advance from reconnaissance to persistent, privileged access to a target organization in a matter of minutes.”

“ARTEX does not introduce new attack techniques; it uses common, pre-existing penetration testing tools and techniques. ARTEX does sometimes transmit distinctive characteristics associated with its integrated penetration testing tools,” Microsoft explained.

“However, threat actors using ARTEX can and do vary network indicators like source IP address or user agent string, especially to bypass static or simple signature-based de

### FIFA says hackers accessed ‘limited set of personal and business information’
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-25T20:40:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Switzerland-based international governing body for soccer says it's working with relevant authorities and law enforcement to investigate a recent breach.
- Business background / implications: The Switzerland-based international governing body for soccer says it's working with relevant authorities and law enforcement to investigate a recent breach.

The Fédération Internationale de Football Association (FIFA) confirmed that internal data was stolen during a recent cyberattack.

The Switzerland-based soccer organization said hackers breached its systems and gained “unauthorised access to a limited set of personal and business information.”

"FIFA is working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement,” a spokesperson said.

"FIFA condemns in the strongest possible terms the unlawful acquisition and publication of information, particularly personal data. Such actions constitute a serious and unacceptable violation of privacy and can have significant consequences for the individuals concerned."

The organization did not respond to follow up questions about the nature of the incident. FIFA is the governing body of international soccer and organizes the World Cup, which was held this summer. It has about 700 employees and is headquartered in Zurich.

The Handsome Boy Hacking Club (HBHC) cybercriminal group claimed last week that it stole more than 12 GB of data from the organization and attempted to extort FIFA but failed.

The group eventually published the stolen data on a cybercriminal forum, claiming to have accessed FIFA’s Microsoft environment. The stolen data allegedly includes identity and immigration documents, financial records, legal documents, governance information and more.

The hacking group emerged last week and little is known about its members or associations.

The FIFA incident follows other recent attacks on European soccer leagues. Spain’s top soccer league LaLiga said on Monday that hackers stole personal information belonging to fans in a cyberattack affecting its digital services and those of several Spanish clubs.

In May, Dutch police arrested a 35-year-old man suspected of hacking into the computer systems of Ajax, one of the Netherlands’ most prominent soccer clubs. The French Football Federation also disclosed a cyberattack in 2025.

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

### 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-25T18:49:52Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The password manager maker said an attacker gained access to an employee's corporate account and took some information about customers.
- Business background / implications: The password manager maker said an attacker gained access to an employee's corporate account and took some information about customers.

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission . This doesn’t affect our editorial independence.

Zack Whittaker is the security editor at TechCrunch. He also authors the weekly cybersecurity newsletter, this week in security .

- What it does: It securely stores passwords, passkeys, software licences and other sensitive information so people and teams can access them without reusing passwords.

### DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-25T17:36:51Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: U.S. prosecutors accused the former executive of defrauding victims of ransomware by paying the hackers, charging victims more, and pocketing the difference.
- Business background / implications: U.S. prosecutors accused the former executive of defrauding victims of ransomware by paying the hackers, charging victims more, and pocketing the difference.

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors , Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCru

### Florida ransomware recovery CEO indicted for allegedly paying hacker ransoms
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-25T17:30:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The CEO of a ransomware recovery firm was indicted by a federal grand jury over allegations he lied about his services and paid out ransoms to cybercriminals.
- Business background / implications: The CEO of a ransomware recovery firm was indicted by a federal grand jury over allegations he lied about his services and paid out ransoms to cybercriminals.

The CEO of a ransomware recovery firm was indicted by a federal grand jury after prosecutors accused him of lying about his services and simply paying out ransoms to cybercriminals.

Colombian national Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, is the co-founder of a company based in Florida that marketed itself as a data recovery service for ransomware victims.

Palacio claimed he had decryption tools that allowed him to help victims without negotiating or paying ransomware gangs. Instead, Palacio allegedly paid ransoms and charged his customers enough to make a profit.

Federal agencies have increasingly sought to crack down on the murky industry of ransomware recovery and cybercriminal mediation in recent months.

“As alleged, the defendant preyed on ransomware victims by falsely marketing a solution he did not possess, secretly negotiating with cybercriminals, and profiting from the difference,” said Charles Calenda, first assistant U.S. attorney.

“Businesses and individuals seeking to recover from a cyberattack should not be victimized for a second time by those they turn to for help.”

He is facing three counts of wire fraud, which carry a maximum sentence of 60 years in prison.

Before the DOJ announced the charges, the news outlet 404 Media and independent journalist Brian Krebs published details of Escobar’s operation, writing that he ran a company called Ransomwarehelp.com. The company’s website currently says it is under maintenance.

On Wednesday, Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva confirmed previous reports that the FBI staged an operation in June where they posed as a ransomware gang and victim.

The FBI went to Palacio for assistance and was told his company “never negotiated with or paid the ransom to ransomware attackers but instead could recover the data through its sophisticated decryption technology.”

“The indictment alleges that despite these representations, Palacio did negotiate with and make a payment in the amount of approximately $6,000 in Bitcoin to the purported ransomware actor, which was also an undercover FBI operative, in exchange for the decryption key,” prosecutors said.

“Palacio charged the undercover company approximately $13,260 for his company’s purported decryption technology services.”

He was arrested in Las Vegas on October 22 before being transferred to Rho

### Australia passes tough legislation requiring big tech to preempt foreseeable harms
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-25T17:07:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Digital Duty of Care Act sailed through Australia's Parliament. It will mandate that online providers establish protocols to protect users from threats of violence and rape as well as behavior that could cause significant harms.
- Business background / implications: The Digital Duty of Care Act sailed through Australia's Parliament. It will mandate that online providers establish protocols to protect users from threats of violence and rape as well as behavior that could cause significant harms.

Australia’s Parliament on Wednesday greenlit legislation creating sweeping online safety reforms that open the possibility of billions of dollars in fines for big tech companies that fail to preempt online harms before they occur.

Known as the Digital Duty of Care Act, the legislation sailed through Parliament with strong backing from Prime Minister Anthony Albanese.

His administration has been a global leader in fighting against online safety harms and spearheaded Australia’s pioneering strict age requirements for social media use that took effect in December 2025 and have since been copied by governments worldwide.

The pending law will mandate that online providers establish protocols to ensure Australians are protected from threats of violence and rape as well as behavior that could cause significant harms.

Platforms are required to detect and decrease risks from harmful content and must bolster protections for users under 18 against pornography, suicide and eating disorders. They also must undertake annual written risk assessments and call out major platform design changes such as endless scrolling that create new harms.

Under the law, users also will be able to opt out of algorithmic feeds and will be required to prove their age to access porn.

After the law passed, Albanese reportedly told Parliament it will be a “powerful instrument to protect our children and our society from online harms by placing the onus on the tech platforms to ensure a safer online environment.”

“This is a victory for our children and young people. The dangers were unforeseen when the digital world was young,” Albanese said. “But now that the digital world has matured, they can be easily recognized and they can be remedied.”

Similar legislation is already in place in the U.K. and European Union . A bill seeking to impose a duty of care against large platforms has been introduced by U.S. lawmakers but has stagnated in Congress.

U.S. officials have accused the Australian legislation of violating free speech, threatening American companies’ business models and imposing vague requirements that will cause tech giants to overhaul their services to avoid liability.

### Allstate suffers multiday tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-25T15:08:37Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The disruption comes at a particularly bad time for the insurance giant, as millions of Americans prepare to travel for the Thanksgiving holiday weekend.
- Business background / implications: The disruption comes at a particularly bad time for the insurance giant, as millions of Americans prepare to travel for the Thanksgiving holiday weekend.

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams , the airlines , Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints , asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector , a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.

Alls

### Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-25T14:20:28Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Todoist said a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates.
- Business background / implications: Todoist said a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates.

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X , and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue .

### Suspected ransomware attack disrupts cancer treatments, surgeries at Guadeloupe hospital
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-25T13:30:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: France’s national cybersecurity agency is investigating a suspected ransomware attack that impaired the computer systems of the University Hospital of Guadeloupe, forcing doctors to postpone surgeries, suspend some cancer treatments and revert to pen and paper.
- Business background / implications: France’s national cybersecurity agency is investigating a suspected ransomware attack that impaired the computer systems of the University Hospital of Guadeloupe, forcing doctors to postpone surgeries, suspend some cancer treatments and revert to pen and paper.

France’s national cybersecurity agency, known as ANSSI, said Tuesday the incident appeared to involve ransomware and that investigators were examining whether hackers gained access to the hospital’s systems through an unnamed IT supplier that was reportedly compromised several months ago.

French prosecutors have launched a criminal investigation into the incident. It remains unclear who was behind the attack. The hospital’s management told local media on Monday that it had not received a ransom demand and ruled out paying a ransom.

The hospital — the largest on the French Caribbean island of approximately 380,000 residents — said on its website it had canceled some surgical procedures, medical consultations and imaging tests through at least Friday.

Radiation therapy has been temporarily suspended over safety concerns, while chemotherapy sessions scheduled for Monday were canceled. The hospital is reviewing upcoming appointments based on each patient’s medical needs.

Emergency services, dialysis treatments and urgent cardiac procedures remain available. The hospital has asked residents to visit its emergency departments only if they need immediate medical care.

Hospital employees first experienced computer and telephone outages on Saturday evening, according to local media reports. What initially appeared to be a technical malfunction turned out to be a cyberattack that encrypted the hospital’s databases.

“We’re back to pen and paper, relying on couriers who travel from one department to another,” the hospital’s director told local media.

As of Tuesday, healthcare workers were still unable to access patients’ electronic medical records and were manually processing prescriptions and collecting laboratory results, slowing patient care.

The hospital has encouraged patients to bring paper copies of their medical records, prescriptions and previous test results to appointments.

It remains unclear whether the hackers stole any personal or medical information. The hospital said it would notify anyone whose data was compromised and warned patients to watch out for phishing attempts by criminals posing as hospital staff.

### FinCEN investigates cybersecurity incident, takes systems offline
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-25T13:03:24Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Treasury financial intelligence unit said the filing portal remains operational, and deadlines for affected filers have been extended. The post FinCEN investigates cybersecurity incident, takes systems offline appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Treasury’s FinCEN took systems offline to investigate an unauthorized intrusion while keeping its Bank Secrecy Act filing portal operational.

The Treasury Department’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network is investigating a cybersecurity incident in which an unauthorized party gained access to a limited subset of its systems, the agency said in a notice posted to its Bank Secrecy Act filing portal.

FinCEN, the federal government’s financial intelligence unit, said it took some systems offline as a precaution while the investigation continues. The agency did not say how the intrusion occurred, how long it lasted or who was behind it. A source told CyberScoop that the FBI, CISA, and third-party forensic examiners are working to determine the scope and impact of the incident. As of today, there is no evidence of any breach of beneficial ownership databases.

The BSA E-Filing System, the portal at the center of the disruption, is FinCEN’s web-based platform for submitting reports required under the Bank Secrecy Act, the 1970 law that forms the backbone of U.S. anti-money-laundering policy. Banks, credit unions, brokerages, casinos and other financial institutions use it to transmit suspicious activity reports, currency transaction reports and related filings electronically through a secure network.

FinCEN said the filing website itself remains operational, though some services may be temporarily unavailable. The agency said it is extending deadlines for filers affected by the disruption.

The agency also said its Financial Institutions Hotline is unaffected by the disruption and remains available to financial institutions that need to expedite delivery of suspicious activity information to law enforcement.

The incident places a temporary strain on infrastructure that the financial sector relies on to meet statutory reporting obligations. Suspicious activity reports filed through the system feed the database that FinCEN uses to support criminal, tax and national security investigations, and delays in filings can slow the flow of information to federal authorities.

It was not immediately clear how long the affected systems would remain offline. FinCEN said it is working to return all systems to normal operations as soon as possible. The agency has not said whether any data was taken or altered.

The incident was announced the same day the CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence discovered a data breach of an open-source intelligence analysis

### Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T22:01:23Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: CyberScoop confirmed the incident, with a source saying “no other systems are currently at risk.” The post Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: The CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence confirmed a data breach affecting an unclassified open-source intelligence system.

The CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence have discovered a data breach of an open-source intelligence analysis system, CyberScoop has confirmed.

The New York Times first reported the breach Tuesday, writing that the breach had exposed some unclassified information. A source familiar with the breach said there was a disruption, but the exposure was limited.

‘The hack did not involve our classified networks or classified information, and no other systems are currently at risk,” the source said.

The discovery is the latest in a series of intrusions affecting U.S. security agencies, including two at the FBI and another at the National Security Agency .

Ben Bernstein, manager of cybersecurity advisers at Huntress, said in an emailed comment that while the reported breach didn’t hit classified data, open-source (OSINT) information can still be sensitive.

“Calling this ‘unclassified’ doesn’t make it harmless,” he said. “These systems pull in public and commercial info like news, satellite imagery, and paid data feeds, so the raw material isn’t a state secret. The value for an adversary is seeing what the CIA and ODNI are doing with it.”

“If you can see what analysts are searching for and what topics they’re tracking, you get a solid look at U.S. intelligence priorities and blind spots without ever touching a classified file,” he continued. “These OSINT networks are also tough to secure because they have to stay open to the public internet to do their job.”

The CIA declined to comment and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence did not immediately respond to a request for comment Tuesday.

## Behavior-based detection engineering

Generic behavior hunts are maintained separately and are not attributed to a specific campaign without source evidence:
- [Suspicious PowerShell/process execution](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_suspicious_execution.kql)
- [Scheduled-task/service persistence](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_persistence_changes.kql)
- [Entra privileged role and credential changes](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/entra_privilege_changes.kql)

## Collection errors

- CERT-In index: TimeoutError: The read operation timed out

## Analyst workflow

1. Match KEV/advisories to asset inventory, versions and internet exposure.
2. Verify exploit status, affected versions and remediation against primary vendor/CERT advisories.
3. Derive behavior hunts from documented TTPs; map ATT&CK only when supported by evidence.
4. Verify candidate IOCs against original source, freshness and local telemetry.
5. Keep queries Draft until schema, syntax, positive/negative tests and tuning are recorded.

## Limitations

- Broad public-source baseline, not exhaustive global intelligence.
- ThreatFox, OTX, GreyNoise and Shadowserver are not enabled; review credentials and provider terms before integration.
- Business discovery uses a wider 7-day window; Google News results are reported leads and require company-announcement or filing verification.
- Feed failures and quiet periods do not mean absence of threats; report timestamps are IST and machine timestamps in JSON are UTC.

Raw evidence: [JSON snapshot](../../../snapshots/2026/11/26.json).
